Stability of Minkowski spacetime in exterior regions
Dawei Shen

Abstract
In 1993, the global stability of Minkowski spacetime has been proven in the celebrated work of Christodoulou and Klainerman [ 5 ] in a maximal foliation. In 2003, Klainerman and Nicolò [ 14 ] gave a second proof of the stability of Minkowski in the case of the exterior of an outgoing null cone. In this paper, we give a new proof of [ 14 ] . Compared to [ 14 ] , we reduce the number of derivatives needed in the proof, simplify the treatment of the last slice, and provide a unified treatment of the decay of initial data. Also, concerning the treatment of curvature estimates, we replace the vectorfield method used in [ 14 ] by the r p r^{p} –weighted estimates of Dafermos and Rodnianski [ 7 ] .



Keywords:

double null foliation, geodesic foliation, Minkowski stability, Peeling properties,
r^{p}
–weighted estimates

1
Introduction

1.1
Einstein vacuum equations and the Cauchy problem

A Lorentzian
4
–manifold
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
is called a vacuum spacetime if it solves the Einstein vacuum equations:

{\mathbf{Ric}}(\mathbf{g})=0\quad\mbox{ in }\mathcal{M},

(1.1)

where
{\mathbf{Ric}}
denotes the Ricci tensor of the Lorentzian metric
\mathbf{g}
. The Einstein vacuum
equations are invariant under diffeomorphisms, and therefore one considers equivalence
classes of solutions. Expressed in general coordinates, (1.1) is a non-linear geometric coupled system of partial differential equations of order 2 for
\mathbf{g}
. In suitable coordinates, for example so-called wave coordinates, it can be shown that (
1.1
) is hyperbolic and hence admits an initial value formulation.

The corresponding initial data for the Einstein vacuum equations is given by specifying a
triplet
(\Sigma,g,k)
where
(\Sigma,g)
is a Riemannian
3
–manifold and
k
is the traceless symmetric
2
–tensor on
\Sigma
satisfying the constraint equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}R&=|k|^{2}-(\tr k)^{2},\\
D^{j}k_{ij}&=D_{i}(\tr k),\end{split}

(1.2)

where
R
denotes the scalar curvature of
g
,
D
denotes the Levi-Civita connection of
g
and

\displaystyle|k|^{2}:=g^{ad}g^{bc}k_{ab}k_{cd},\qquad\quad\tr k:=g^{ij}k_{ij}.

In the future development
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
of such initial data
(\Sigma,g,k)
,
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{M}
is a spacelike hypersurface with induced metric
g
and second fundamental form
k
.

The seminal well-posedness results for the Cauchy problem obtained in
[
3
,
4
]
ensure that for any smooth Cauchy data, there exists a unique smooth maximal globally hyperbolic development
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
solution of Einstein equations (
1.1
) such that
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{M}
and
g
,
k
are respectively the first and second fundamental forms of
\Sigma
in
\mathcal{M}
.

The prime example of a vacuum spacetime is Minkowski space:

\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{R}^{4},\qquad\mathbf{g}=-dt^{2}+(dx^{1})^{2}+(dx^{2})^{2}+(dx^{3})^{2},

for which Cauchy data are given by

\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{3},\qquad g=(dx^{1})^{2}+(dx^{2})^{2}+(dx^{3})^{2},\qquad k=0.

In the present work, we consider the problem of the stability of Minkowski spacetime and start with reviewing the literature on this problem.

1.2
Previous works of the stability of Minkowski spacetime

In 1993, Christodoulou and Klainerman
[
5
]
proved the stability of Minkowski for the Einstein-vacuum equations, a milestone in the domain of mathematical general relativity. In 2003, Klainerman and Nicolò
[
14
]
gave a second proof of this result in the exterior of an outgoing cone. Moreover, Klainerman and Nicolò
[
15
]
showed that under stronger asymptotic decay and regularity properties than those used in
[
5
,
14
]
, asymptotically flat initial data sets lead to solutions of the Einstein vacuum equations which have strong peeling properties. Given that the goal of this paper is to provide a new proof of the stability of Minkowski in the exterior region, we will state the results of
[
5
]
and
[
14
]
in section
1.3
.

The proofs in
[
5
]
and
[
14
]
are based respectively on the maximal foliation and the double null foliation. Lindblad and Rodnianski
[
21
,
22
]
gave a new proof of the stability of the Minkowski spacetime using
wave-coordinates
and showing that the Einstein equations verify the so called
weak null structure
in that gauge. Bieri
[
1
]
gave a proof requiring less derivative and less vectorfield compared to
[
5
]
. Huneau
[
12
]
proved the nonlinear stability of Minkowski spacetime with a translation Killing field using generalised wave-coordinates. Using the framework of Melrose’s b-analysis, Hintz and Vasy
[
10
]
reproved the stability of Minkowski space. Graf
[
9
]
proved the global nonlinear stability of Minkowski space in the context of the spacelike-characteristic Cauchy problem for Einstein vacuum equations, which together with
[
14
]
allows to reobtain
[
5
]
.

There are also stability results concerning Einstein’s equations coupled with non trivial matter fields:

•

Einstein-Maxwell system: Zipser
[
31
]
extended the framework of
[
5
]
to show the stability of the Minkowski spacetime solution to the Einstein–Maxwell system. In
[
24
]
, Loizelet used the framework of
[
21
,
22
]
to demonstrate the stability of the Minkowski spacetime solution of the Einstein-scalar field-Maxwell system in
(1+n)
-dimensions
(n\geq 3)
. Speck
[
26
]
gave a proof of the global nonlinear stability of the
(1+3)
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime solution to the coupled system for a family of electromagnetic fields, which includes the standard Maxwell fields.

•

Einstein-Klein-Gordon system: Lefloch and Ma
[
20
]
and Wang
[
29
]
proved the global stability of Minkowski for the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system with initial data coinciding with the Schwarzschild solution with small mass outside a compact set. Ionescu and Pausader
[
13
]
proved the global stability of Minkowski for the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system for general initial data.

•

Einstein-Vlasov system: Taylor
[
27
]
considered the massless case where the initial data for the Vlasov part is compactly supported on the mass shell. Fajman, Joudioux and Smulevici
[
8
]
considered the massive case where the initial data coincides with Schwarzschild in the exterior region and with compact support assumption only in space on the Vlasov part. Lindblad and Taylor
[
23
]
considered the massive case where the initial data has compact support for the Vlasov part. Bigorgne, Fajman, Joudioux, Smulevici and Thaller
[
2
]
considered the massless case for general initial data. Wang
[
30
]
considered the massive case for general initial data.

1.3
Minkowski Stability in
[
5
]
and
[
14
]

We recall in this section the results in
[
5
,
14
]
. First, we recall the definition of a
maximal hypersurface
, which plays an important role in the statements of the main theorems in
[
5
,
14
]
.

Definition 1.1
.

An initial data
(\Sigma,g,k)
is posed on a maximal hypersurface if it satisfies

\tr k=0.

(1.3)

In this case, we say that
(\Sigma,g,k)
is a maximal initial data set, and the constraint equations (
1.2
) reduce to

R=|k|^{2},\qquad\sdiv k=0,\qquad\tr k=0.

(1.4)

We introduce the notion of
s
–asymptotically flat initial data
.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
s
be a real number with
s>3
. We say that a data set
(\Sigma_{0},g,k)
is
s
–asymptotically flat if there exists a coordinate system
(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
defined outside a sufficiently large compact set such that
1
1

1

The notation
f=o_{l}(r^{-m})
means
\partial^{\alpha}f=o(r^{-m-|\alpha|})
,
|\alpha|\leq l
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}g_{ij}=\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)^{-1}dr^{2}+r^{2}d\sigma_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}+o_{4}(r^{-\frac{s-1}{2}}),\\
k_{ij}=o_{3}(r^{-\frac{s+1}{2}}).\end{split}

(1.5)

We also introduce the following functional associated to any asymptotically flat initial data set:

J_{0}(\Sigma_{0},g,k):=\sup_{\Sigma_{0}}\Big((d_{0}^{2}+1)^{3}|\sRic|^{2}\Big)+\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\sum_{l=0}^{3}(d_{0}^{2}+1)^{l+1}|D^{l}k|^{2}+\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\sum_{l=0}^{1}(d_{0}^{2}+1)^{l+3}|D^{l}B|^{2},

(1.6)

where
d_{0}
is the geodesic distance from a fixed point
O\in\Sigma_{0}
, and
B_{ij}:=(\curl\widehat{\overline{R}})_{ij}
is the
Bach tensor
,
\widehat{\overline{R}}
is the traceless part of
\sRic
. Now, we can state the main theorems of
[
5
]
and
[
14
]
.

Theorem 1.3

(Global stability of Minkowski space
[
5
]
)
.

There exists an
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small such that if
J_{0}(\Sigma_{0},g,k)\leq\epsilon^{2}
, then the initial data set
(\Sigma_{0},g,k)
,
4
–asymptotically flat (in the sense of Definition
1.2
) and maximal, has a unique, globally hyperbolic, smooth, geodesically complete solution. This development is globally asymptotically flat, i.e. the Riemann curvature tensor tends to zero along any causal or space-like geodesic. Moreover, there exists a global maximal time function
t
and an optical function
u
2
2

2

An optical function
u
is a scalar function satisfying
\mathbf{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}u\partial_{\beta}u=0
.
defined everywhere in an external region.

Theorem 1.4

(Minkowski Stability in the exterior region
[
14
]
)
.

Consider an initial data set
(\Sigma_{0},g,k)
,
4
–asymptotically flat and maximal, and assume
J_{0}(\Sigma_{0},g,k)
is bounded. Then, given a sufficiently large compact set
K\subset\Sigma_{0}
such that
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\overline{B}_{1}
, and under additional smallness assumptions, there exists a unique development
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
with the following properties:

(1)
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
can be foliated by a double null foliation
\{C_{u}\}
and
\{{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}\}
whose outgoing leaves
C_{u}
are complete.

(2) We have detailed control of all the quantities associated with the double null foliations of the spacetime, see Theorem 3.7.1 of
[
14
]
.

Remark 1.5
.

[
15
]
extends Theorem
1.4
to the case
s>7
by assuming sufficient regular initial data
(\Sigma_{0},g,k)
.

The goal of this paper is to reprove
[
14
]
by a different method and to treat more general asymptotic behavior than
s=4
in (
1.5
).

1.4
Rough version of the main theorem

In this section, we state a simple version of our main theorem. For the explicit statement, see Theorem
3.1
.

Theorem 1.6

(Main Theorem (first version))
.

Let
s>3
, and let an initial data set
(\Sigma_{0},g,k)
which is
s
–asymptotically flat in the sense of Definition
1.2
. Let a sufficiently large compact set
K\subset\Sigma_{0}
such that
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\overline{B}_{1}
. Assume that we have a smallness conditions in an initial layer region
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
3
3

3

The initial data layer
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
is defined in section
2.1.2
.
near
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
. Then, there exists a unique future development
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
in its future domain of dependence with the following properties:

•

(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
can be foliated by a double null foliation
(C_{u},{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}})
whose outgoing leaves
C_{u}
are complete for all
u\leq u_{0}
;

•

We have detailed control of all the quantities associated with the double null foliations of the spacetime, see Theorem
3.1
.

Remark 1.7
.

In the particular case
s=4
, we reobtain the results of
[
14
]
. Also, in the case
s>7
, we reobtain the strong peeling properties of
[
15
]
.

The proof of Theorem
1.6
has the same structure as in
[
14
]
, see section
3.4
. Below, we compare the proof of this paper and that of
[
14
]
:

1.

In
[
14
]
, the functional (
1.6
) is introduced to fix the initial conditions on the initial hypersurface. Here we fix the initial conditions in an initial layer region
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
near the initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
.

2.

To estimate the norms of curvature components,
[
14
]
uses the vectorfield method introduced in
[
5
]
. Here, we estimate the curvature norms by
r^{p}
-weighted estimate, a method introduced by Dafermos and Rodnianski in
[
7
]
. This allows for a simpler treatment of the curvature estimates.

3.

[
14
]
uses second order derivatives of curvature and third order derivatives of Ricci coefficients. Thanks to the use of
r^{p}
–weighted estimates, we only need first order derivatives of both curvature and Ricci coefficients.

4.

In order to control one more derivative for Ricci coefficients compared to curvature,
[
14
]
relies on the canonical foliation on the last slice
{\underline{C}}_{*}
. Since we control the same number of derivatives of curvature and Ricci coefficients, instead of introducing the canonical foliation, we use the geodesic foliation to simplify the estimates on the last slice
{\underline{C}}_{*}
.

1.5
Structure of the paper

•

In section
2
, we recall the fundamental notions and the basic equations.

•

In section
3
, we present the main theorem. We then state intermediate results, and prove the main theorem. The rest of the paper focuses on the proof of these intermediary results. In sections
4
–
6
, we consider the case
s\in[4,6]
and deal with the other cases in Appendices
B
and
C
.

•

In section
4
, we apply
r^{p}
–weighted estimates to Bianchi equations to control curvature.

•

In section
5
, we estimate the Ricci coefficients using the null structure equations.

•

In section
6
, we estimate the norms of curvature components and Ricci coefficients in the initial layer region
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
and on the last slice
{\underline{C}}_{*}
. We also show how to extend the spacetime in the context of a bootstrap argument.

•

In Appendix
A
, we prove the equivalence between optical functions and area radius associated to various frames used in the proof.

•

In Appendix
B
, we prove Theorem
1.6
in the case
s\in(3,4)
.

•

In Appendix
C
, we prove Theorem
1.6
in the case
s>6
by applying the
r^{p}
–weighted method to the Teukolsky equation of
\alpha
.

1.6
Acknowledgements

The author is very grateful to Jérémie Szeftel for his support, discussions, encouragements and patient guidance.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Geometric set-up

2.1.1
Double null foliation

We first introduce the geometric setup. We denote
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
a spacetime
\mathcal{M}
with the Lorentzian metric
\mathbf{g}
and
\mathbf{D}
its Levi-Civita connection. Let
u
and
{\underline{u}}
be two optical functions on
\mathcal{M}
, that is

\mathbf{g}(\grad u,\grad u)=\mathbf{g}(\grad{\underline{u}},\grad{\underline{u}})=0.

The spacetime
\mathcal{M}
is foliated by the level sets of
u
and
{\underline{u}}
respectively, and the functions
u,{\underline{u}}
are required to increase towards the future. We use
C_{u}
to denote the outgoing null hypersurfaces which are the level sets of
u
and use
{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}
to denote the incoming null hypersurfaces which are the level sets of
{\underline{u}}
. We denote

S(u,{\underline{u}}):=C_{u}\cap{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}},

(2.1)

which are space-like
2
–spheres. We introduce the vectorfields
L
and
\underline{L}
by

L:=-\grad u,\quad\underline{L}:=-\grad{\underline{u}}.

We define a positive function
\Omega
by the formula

\mathbf{g}(L,\underline{L})=-\frac{1}{2\Omega^{2}},

where
\Omega
is called the lapse function. We then define the normalized null pair
(e_{3},e_{4})
by

e_{3}=2\Omega\underline{L},\quad e_{4}=2\Omega L,

and define another null pair by

\underline{N}=\Omega e_{3},\quad N=\Omega e_{4}.

On a given two sphere
S(u,{\underline{u}})
, we choose a local frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
, we call
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
a null frame. As a convention, throughout the paper, we use capital Latin letters
A,B,C,...
to denote an index from 1 to 2 and Greek letters
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,...
to denote an index from 1 to 4, e.g.
e_{A}
denotes either
e_{1}
or
e_{2}
.

The spacetime metric
\mathbf{g}
induces a Riemannian metric
\gamma
on
S(u,{\underline{u}})
. We use
\nabla
to denote the Levi-Civita connection of
\gamma
on
S(u,{\underline{u}})
. Using
(u,{\underline{u}})
, we introduce a local coordinate system
(u,{\underline{u}},\phi^{A})
on
\mathcal{M}
with
e_{4}(\phi^{A})=0
. In that coordinates system, the metric
\mathbf{g}
takes the form:

\mathbf{g}=-2\Omega^{2}(d{\underline{u}}\otimes du+du\otimes d{\underline{u}})+\gamma_{AB}(d\phi^{A}-\underline{b}^{A}du)\otimes(d\phi^{B}-\underline{b}^{B}du),

(2.2)

and we have

\underline{N}=\partial_{u}+\underline{b},\qquad N=\partial_{{\underline{u}}},\qquad\underline{b}:=\underline{b}^{A}\partial_{\phi^{A}}.

We recall the null decomposition of the Ricci coefficients and curvature components of the null frame
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4})
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\underline{\chi}_{AB}&=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{A}e_{3},e_{B}),\qquad\quad\chi_{AB}=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{A}e_{4},e_{B}),\\
\underline{\xi}_{A}&=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{3},e_{A}),\qquad\quad\,\,\xi_{A}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{4},e_{A}),\\
\underline{\omega}&=\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{3},e_{4}),\qquad\quad\,\,\,\,\,\omega=\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{4},e_{3}),\\
\underline{\eta}_{A}&=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{3},e_{A}),\qquad\quad\,\eta_{A}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{4},e_{A}),\\
\zeta_{A}&=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{D}_{e_{A}}e_{4},e_{3}),\end{split}

(2.3)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{AB}&={\mathbf{R}}(e_{A},e_{4},e_{B},e_{4}),\qquad\,\,\,\underline{\alpha}_{AB}={\mathbf{R}}(e_{A},e_{3},e_{B},e_{3}),\\
\beta_{A}&=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{R}}(e_{A},e_{4},e_{3},e_{4}),\qquad\,\,\;\underline{\beta}_{A}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{R}}(e_{A},e_{3},e_{3},e_{4}),\\
\rho&=\frac{1}{4}{\mathbf{R}}(e_{3},e_{4},e_{3},e_{4}),\qquad\,\;\;\,\,\;\,\sigma=\frac{1}{4}{{}^{*}{\mathbf{R}}}(e_{3},e_{4},e_{3},e_{4}),\end{split}

(2.4)

where
{}^{*}{\mathbf{R}}
denotes the Hodge dual of
{\mathbf{R}}
. The null second fundamental forms
\chi,\underline{\chi}
are further decomposed in their traces
\tr\chi
and
\tr\underline{\chi}
, and traceless parts
\widehat{\chi}
and
\widehat{\underline{\chi}}
:

\displaystyle\tr\chi

\displaystyle:=\delta^{AB}\chi_{AB},\qquad\quad\,\widehat{\chi}_{AB}:=\chi_{AB}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{AB}\tr\chi,

\displaystyle\tr\underline{\chi}

\displaystyle:=\delta^{AB}\underline{\chi}_{AB},\qquad\quad\,\widehat{\underline{\chi}}_{AB}:=\underline{\chi}_{AB}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{AB}\tr\underline{\chi}.

We define the horizontal covariant operator
\nabla
as follows:

\nabla_{X}Y:=\mathbf{D}_{X}Y-\frac{1}{2}\underline{\chi}(X,Y)e_{4}-\frac{1}{2}\chi(X,Y)e_{3}.

We also define
\nabla_{4}X
and
\nabla_{3}X
to be the horizontal projections:

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}X

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{D}_{4}X-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(X,\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{3})e_{4}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(X,\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{4})e_{3},

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}X

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{D}_{3}X-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(X,\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{3})e_{3}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{g}(X,\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{4})e_{4}.

A tensor field
\psi
defined on
\mathcal{M}
is called tangent to
S
if it is a priori defined on the spacetime
\mathcal{M}
and all the possible contractions of
\psi
with either
e_{3}
or
e_{4}
are zero. We use
\nabla_{3}\psi
and
\nabla_{4}\psi
to denote the projection to
S(u,{\underline{u}})
of usual derivatives
\mathbf{D}_{3}\psi
and
\mathbf{D}_{4}\psi
. As a direct consequence of (
2.3
), we have the Ricci formulas:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{D}_{A}e_{B}&=\nabla_{A}e_{B}+\frac{1}{2}\chi_{AB}e_{3}+\frac{1}{2}\underline{\chi}_{AB}e_{4},\\
\mathbf{D}_{A}e_{3}&=\underline{\chi}_{AB}e_{B}+\zeta_{A}e_{3},\\
\mathbf{D}_{A}e_{4}&=\chi_{AB}e_{B}-\zeta_{A}e_{4},\\
\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{A}&=\nabla_{3}e_{A}+\eta_{A}e_{3}+\underline{\xi}_{A}e_{4},\\
\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{A}&=\nabla_{4}e_{A}+\underline{\eta}_{A}e_{4}+\xi_{A}e_{4},\\
\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{3}&=-2\underline{\omega}e_{3}+2\underline{\xi}_{B}e_{B},\\
\mathbf{D}_{3}e_{4}&=2\underline{\omega}e_{4}+2\eta_{B}e_{B},\\
\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{4}&=-2\omega e_{4}+2\xi_{B}e_{B},\\
\mathbf{D}_{4}e_{3}&=2\omega e_{3}+2\underline{\eta}_{B}e_{B}.\end{split}

(2.5)

In addition to

\xi=\underline{\xi}=0,

(2.6)

the following identities hold for a double null foliation:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla\log\Omega&=\frac{1}{2}(\eta+\underline{\eta}),\qquad\;\;\;\,\omega=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{D}_{4}(\log\Omega),\qquad\;\;\;\,\underline{\omega}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{D}_{3}(\log\Omega),\\
\eta&=\zeta+\nabla\log\Omega,\qquad\underline{\eta}=-\zeta+\nabla\log\Omega,\end{split}

(2.7)

see for example (6) in
[
17
]
.

2.1.2
Initial layer region

Let
\Sigma_{0}
be a spacelike hypersurface. Let
K\subset\Sigma_{0}
be a compact subset such that
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\overline{B_{1}}
where
\overline{B_{1}}
is the unit closed ball in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We fix a foliation on the initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
by the level sets of a scalar function
w
. The leaves are denoted by

S_{(0)}(w_{1})=\{p\in\Sigma_{0}/\,w(p)=w_{1}\},

(2.8)

where
w_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
. We assume that

\partial K=\{p\in\Sigma_{0}/\,w(p)=w_{0}\},\qquad K=\{p\in\Sigma_{0}/\,w(p)\leq w_{0}\},

(2.9)

where
w_{0}
is the area radius of
\partial K
defined by

w_{0}:=\sqrt{\frac{|\partial K|}{4\pi}}.

(2.10)

We can construct a double null foliation in a neighborhood of
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
. For this, we denote
T
the normal vectorfield of
\Sigma_{0}
oriented towards the future and
N
the unit vectorfield tangent to
\Sigma_{0}
, oriented towards infinity and orthogonal to the leaves
S_{0}(w)
. We define two null vectors on
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
by

L_{(0)}:=N(w)(T+N),\qquad\underline{L}:=N(w)(T-N).

(2.11)

We extend the definition of
L_{(0)}
and
\underline{L}
to a neighborhood of
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
by the geodesic equations:

\mathbf{D}_{L_{(0)}}L_{(0)}=0,\qquad\mathbf{D}_{\underline{L}}\underline{L}=0.

(2.12)

The lapse function
\Omega_{(0)}
is defined by

\mathbf{g}(L_{(0)},\underline{L})=-\frac{1}{2{\Omega_{(0)}}^{2}}.

(2.13)

Then, we define two optical functions
u_{(0)}
and
{\underline{u}}
satisfying the initial conditions

u_{(0)}\big|_{\Sigma_{0}\setminus K}=-w,\qquad{\underline{u}}\big|_{\Sigma_{0}\setminus K}=w,

and the equations

L_{(0)}=-\grad u_{(0)},\qquad\underline{L}=-\grad{\underline{u}}.

Recall that
u_{(0)}+{\underline{u}}=0
on
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
, we define the region

\mathcal{K}_{(0)}:=\{p/\,0\leq u_{(0)}(p)+{\underline{u}}(p)\leq 2\delta_{0}\},

(2.14)

where
0<\delta_{0}<1
is a constant. We call
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
the
initial layer region
of height
\delta_{0}
, the double null foliation
(u_{(0)},{\underline{u}})
is called the
initial layer foliation
. Its leaves are denoted by

S_{(0)}(u_{(0)},{\underline{u}}):=(C_{(0)})_{u_{(0)}}\cap{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}.

We define

\displaystyle w:=\frac{{\underline{u}}-u_{(0)}}{2}\quad\mbox{ in }\mathcal{K}_{(0)},

(2.15)

which extend the definition of
w
from
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
to
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
. Moreover, we transport the coordinates
{\phi}_{(0)}^{A}
from
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
to
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
by
\underline{L}({\phi}_{(0)}^{A})=0
. Hence, we deduce that the metric
\mathbf{g}
in
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
takes the following form in the
({\underline{u}},u_{(0)},\phi_{(0)}^{A})
coordinates system:

\mathbf{g}=-2{\Omega_{(0)}}^{2}(d{\underline{u}}\otimes du_{(0)}+du_{(0)}\otimes d{\underline{u}})+(\gamma_{(0)})_{AB}(d{\phi}_{(0)}^{A}-b^{A}d{\underline{u}})(d{\phi}_{(0)}^{B}-b^{B}d{\underline{u}}),

(2.16)

where

b^{A}=\Omega_{(0)}(e_{4})_{(0)}({\phi}_{(0)}^{A}).

2.1.3
Bootstrap region

Now, we define the bootstrap region and its double null foliation. We choose a value
{\underline{u}}_{*}>w_{0}
, then
w={\underline{u}}_{*}
defines a leaf of
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
, denoted by
S_{0}({\underline{u}}_{*})
. We construct a incoming cone
{\underline{C}}_{*}
called the
last slice
from
S_{0}({\underline{u}}_{*})
. We denote

\underline{L}_{*}:=\underline{L}\big|_{{\underline{C}}_{*}},

(2.17)

which is fixed by (
2.11
) and (
2.12
). Notice that
\underline{L}_{*}
is a generator of
{\underline{C}}_{*}
. We denote
u^{*}
an affine parameter, that is a function defined on
{\underline{C}}_{*}
satisfying

\underline{L}_{*}(u^{*})=2.

(2.18)

Thus,
{\underline{C}}_{*}
is endowed with a geodesic foliation of affine parameter
u^{*}
. For every
u
,
\{u^{*}=u\}
is a sphere on
{\underline{C}}_{*}
. We can therefore construct an outgoing cone from
\{u^{*}=u\}
to the past, denoted by
C_{u}
. Then,
C_{u}\cap\Sigma_{0}
is a sphere on
\Sigma_{0}
, which does a priori not coincide with the leaves of
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
, see Figure
1
.

Figure 1:
The leaves of
\Sigma_{0}

There exists a unique cone
C_{u_{0}}
located outside of the
J^{+}(K)
, the causal future of
K
, but touch the boundary
\partial K
, see Figure
1
. By (
2.18
),
u^{*}
is unique up to a constant. We choose
u_{0}=-w_{0}
where
w_{0}
is defined in (
2.10
). Combining with (
2.18
), this fixes
u^{*}
.

Remark 2.1
.

Remark that we fix
u^{*}
by choosing the value near
\partial K
. We do not have

u^{*}|_{S_{0}({\underline{u}}_{*})}=-{\underline{u}}_{*}.

The reason is that we need to integrate the quantities in the initial layer forward instead of backward. This will lead to a logarithm difference which is sufficient to prove the equivalences, see Appendix
A
.

Now we define the null vector
L
on
{\underline{C}}_{*}
by the relation

\mathbf{g}(L,\underline{L})=-2,

(2.19)

Hence, we have
\Omega=\frac{1}{2}
on
{\underline{C}}_{*}
. By the equations

-\grad u=L,\qquad u|_{{\underline{C}}_{*}}=u^{*},

(2.20)

we can extend
u
to the causal past of
{\underline{C}}_{*}
. So we have constructed a double null foliation
(u,{\underline{u}})
which is geodesic on
{\underline{C}}_{*}
. For every
(u,{\underline{u}})
, we denote

V(u,{\underline{u}}):=J^{-}(S(u,{\underline{u}}))\cap J^{+}(\Sigma_{0}),

(2.21)

where for a domain
\mathcal{M}
,
J^{+}(\mathcal{M})
and resp.
J^{-}(\mathcal{M})
denotes the causal future and resp. causal past of
\mathcal{M}
. We also denote the bootstrap region by

\mathcal{K}:=V(u_{0},{\underline{u}}_{*}).

(2.22)

Remark that
\mathcal{K}
is covered by the double null foliation
(u,{\underline{u}})
, and that the boundaries of
\mathcal{K}
are:

1.

a finite region of
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
;

2.

a portion of an outgoing cone
C_{0}
, where
C_{0}:=\{p\in\mathcal{K}|u(p)=u_{0}\}
;

3.

a portion of the last slice
{\underline{C}}_{*}
.

Note that we have the following types of manifolds in this paper: spacetime regions
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
, initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{0}
, and the spheres
S(u,{\underline{u}})
and
S_{(0)}(u_{(0)},{\underline{u}})
. Every manifold has its metric, Levi-Civita connection and curvature tensor:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{K},\mathbf{g},\mathbf{D},{\mathbf{R}}),\quad(\mathcal{K}_{(0)},\mathbf{g}_{(0)},\mathbf{D}_{(0)},{\mathbf{R}}_{(0)}),\quad(\Sigma_{0},g,D,R),\quad(S,\gamma,\nabla,\mathbf{K}),\quad(S_{(0)},\gamma_{(0)},\nabla_{(0)},\mathbf{K}_{(0)}),

where
\mathbf{K}
(resp.
\mathbf{K}_{(0)}
) is the Gauss curvature of
S
(resp.
S_{(0)}
).

2.2
Integral formulas

We define the
S
-average of scalar functions.

Definition 2.2
.

Given any scalar function
f
, we denote its average and its average free part by

\overline{f}:=\frac{1}{|S|}\int_{S}f\,d\gamma,\qquad\widecheck{f}:=f-\overline{f}.

The following lemma follows immediately from the definition.

Lemma 2.3
.

For any two scalar functions
u
and
v
, we have

\overline{uv}=\overline{u}\,\overline{v}+\overline{\widecheck{u}\widecheck{v}},

and

uv-\overline{uv}=\widecheck{u}\overline{v}+\overline{u}\widecheck{v}+\left(\widecheck{u}\widecheck{v}-\overline{\widecheck{u}\widecheck{v}}\right).

We recall the following integral formulas, which will be used repeatedly in this paper.

Lemma 2.4
.

For any scalar function
f
, the following equations hold:

\displaystyle\Omega e_{4}\left(\int_{S(u,{\underline{u}})}fd\gamma\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{S(u,{\underline{u}})}\left(\Omega e_{4}(f)+\Omega\tr\chi f\right)d\gamma,

\displaystyle\Omega e_{3}\left(\int_{S(u,{\underline{u}})}fd\gamma\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{S(u,{\underline{u}})}\left(\Omega e_{3}(f)+\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}f\right)d\gamma.

Taking
f=1
, we obtain

e_{4}(r)=\frac{\overline{\Omega\tr\chi}}{2\Omega}r,\qquad e_{3}(r)=\frac{\overline{\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}}}{2\Omega}r,

where
r
is the
area radius
defined by

r(u,{\underline{u}}):=\sqrt{\frac{|S(u,{\underline{u}})|}{4\pi}}.

Proof.

See Lemma 2.26 of
[
16
]
or Lemma 3.1.3 of
[
14
]
.
∎

Corollary 2.5
.

For any scalar function
f
, the following equations hold:

\displaystyle\Omega e_{4}(\overline{f})

\displaystyle=\overline{\Omega e_{4}(f)}+\overline{\widecheck{\Omega\tr\chi}\,\widecheck{f}},

\displaystyle\Omega e_{3}(\overline{f})

\displaystyle=\overline{\Omega e_{3}(f)}+\overline{\widecheck{\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}}\,\widecheck{f}}.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
2.4
, we infer

\displaystyle\Omega e_{4}(\overline{f})

\displaystyle=\Omega e_{4}\left(\frac{1}{|S|}\int_{S}fd\gamma\right)=-\frac{\Omega e_{4}(|S|)}{|S|^{2}}\int_{S}fd\gamma+\frac{1}{|S|}\int_{S}\left(\Omega e_{4}(f)+\Omega\tr\chi f\right)d\gamma

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\Omega e_{4}(r)}{r}\overline{f}+\overline{\Omega e_{4}(f)}+\overline{\Omega\tr\chi\,f}=-\overline{\Omega\tr\chi}\,\overline{f}+\overline{\Omega e_{4}(f)}+\overline{\Omega\tr\chi\,f}

\displaystyle=\overline{\Omega e_{4}(f)}+\overline{\widecheck{\Omega\tr\chi}\,\widecheck{f}},

which implies the first identity. The second identity can be obtained similarly. This concludes the proof of Corollary
2.5
.
∎

2.3
Hodge systems

Definition 2.6
.

For tensor fields defined on a
2
–sphere
S
, we denote by
\mathfrak{s}_{0}:=\mathfrak{s}_{0}(S)
the set of pairs of scalar functions,
\mathfrak{s}_{1}:=\mathfrak{s}_{1}(S)
the set of
1
–forms and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}:=\mathfrak{s}_{2}(S)
the set of symmetric traceless
2
–tensors.

Definition 2.7
.

Given
\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
, we define its Hodge dual

{{}^{*}\xi}_{A}:=\in_{AB}\xi^{B}.

Clearly
{}^{*}\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and

{}^{*}(^{*}\xi)=-\xi.

Given
U\in\mathfrak{s}_{2}
, we define its Hodge dual

{{}^{*}U}_{AB}:=\in_{AC}{U^{C}}_{B}.

Observe that
{}^{*}U\in\mathfrak{s}_{2}
and

{}^{*}(^{*}U)=-U.

Definition 2.8
.

Given
\xi,\eta\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
, we denote

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\eta

\displaystyle:=\delta^{AB}\xi_{A}\eta_{B},

\displaystyle\xi\wedge\eta

\displaystyle:=\in^{AB}\xi_{A}\eta_{B},

\displaystyle(\xi\widehat{\otimes}\eta)_{AB}

\displaystyle:=\xi_{A}\eta_{B}+\xi_{B}\eta_{A}-\delta_{AB}\xi\cdot\eta.

Given
\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
,
U\in\mathfrak{s}_{2}
, we denote

\displaystyle(\xi\cdot U)_{A}:=\delta^{BC}\xi_{B}U_{AC}.

Given
U,V\in\mathfrak{s}_{2}
, we denote

\displaystyle(U\wedge V)_{AB}:=\in^{AB}U_{AC}V_{CB}.

Definition 2.9
.

For a given
\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
, we define the following differential operators:

\displaystyle\sdiv\xi

\displaystyle:=\delta^{AB}\nabla_{A}\xi_{B},

\displaystyle\curl\xi

\displaystyle:=\in^{AB}\nabla_{A}\xi_{B},

\displaystyle(\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\xi)_{AB}

\displaystyle:=\nabla_{A}\xi_{B}+\nabla_{B}\xi_{A}-\delta_{AB}(\sdiv\xi).

Definition 2.10
.

We define the following Hodge type operators, as introduced in section 2.2 in
[
5
]
:

•

d_{1}
takes
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
into
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
and is given by:

d_{1}\xi:=(\sdiv\xi,\curl\xi),

•

d_{2}
takes
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
into
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and is given by:

(d_{2}U)_{A}:=\nabla^{B}U_{AB},

•

d_{1}^{*}
takes
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
into
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and is given by:

\displaystyle d_{1}^{*}(f,f_{*})_{A}:=-\nabla_{A}f+\in_{AB}\nabla_{B}f_{*},

•

d_{2}^{*}
takes
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
into
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
and is given by:

\displaystyle d_{2}^{*}\xi:=-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\xi.

We have the following identities:

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{1}^{*}d_{1}&=-\Delta_{1}+\mathbf{K},\qquad\qquad d_{1}d_{1}^{*}=-\Delta_{{0}},\\
d_{2}^{*}d_{2}&=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{2}+\mathbf{K},\qquad\quad d_{2}d_{2}^{*}=-\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{1}+\mathbf{K}).\end{split}

(2.23)

where
\mathbf{K}
denotes the Gauss curvature on
S
. See for example (2.2.2) in
[
5
]
for a proof of (
2.23
).

Definition 2.11
.

We define the weighted angular derivatives
\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}U

\displaystyle:=rd_{2}U,\qquad\forall U\in\mathfrak{s}_{2},

\displaystyle\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}\xi

\displaystyle:=rd_{1}\xi,\qquad\,\,\,\forall\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1},

\displaystyle\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}f

\displaystyle:=rd_{1}^{*}f,\qquad\,\,\forall f\in\mathfrak{s}_{0}.

We denote for any tensor
h\in\mathfrak{s}_{k}
,
k=0,1,2
,

h^{(0)}:=h,\qquad h^{(1)}:=(h,\not{{\mathfrak{d}}}h).

2.4
Elliptic estimates for Hodge systems

Definition 2.12
.

For a tensor field
f
on a
2
–sphere
S
, we denote its
L^{p}
–norm:

|f|_{p,S}:=\left(\int_{S}|f|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(2.24)

Proposition 2.13

(
L^{p}
estimates for Hodge systems)
.

Assume that
S
is an arbitrary compact 2-surface with positive bounded Gauss curvature. Then the following statements hold for all
p\in(1,+\infty)
:

1.

Let
\phi\in\mathfrak{s}_{0}
be a solution of
\Delta\phi=f
. Then we have

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}\phi|_{p,S}+r^{-1}|\nabla\phi|_{p,S}+r^{-2}|\phi-\overline{\phi}|_{p,S}\lesssim|f|_{p,S}.

2.

Let
\xi\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}
be a solution of
d_{1}\xi=(f,f_{*})
. Then we have

\displaystyle|\nabla\xi|_{p,S}+r^{-1}|\xi|_{p,S}\lesssim|(f,f_{*})|_{p,S}.

3.

Let
U\in\mathfrak{s}_{2}
be a solution of
d_{2}U=f
. Then we have

\displaystyle|\nabla U|_{p,S}+r^{-1}|U|_{p,S}\lesssim|f|_{p,S}.

Proof.

See Corollary 2.3.1.1 of
[
5
]
.
∎

2.5
Schematic notation
\Gamma_{g}
,
\Gamma_{b}
and
\Gamma_{a}

We introduce the following schematic notations for the Ricci coefficients.

Definition 2.14
.

We divide the Ricci coefficients into three parts:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{g}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\eta,\,\underline{\eta},\,\zeta,\,\widehat{\chi},\,\widecheck{\Omega\tr\chi},\,\widecheck{\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}},\,\widecheck{\omega},\,\nabla\log\Omega\right\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{b}

\displaystyle:=\{\widehat{}\underline{\chi},\,\widecheck{\underline{\omega}}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{a}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\omega,\,\underline{\omega},\,\tr\chi-\frac{2}{r},\,\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{2}{r}\right\}.

We also denote:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}:=(r\nabla)^{\leq 1}\Gamma_{g}\cup\{r\beta,r\widecheck{\rho},r\sigma\},\qquad\Gamma_{b}^{(1)}:=(r\nabla)^{\leq 1}\Gamma_{b}\cup\{r\underline{\beta}\},\qquad\Gamma_{a}^{(1)}:=(r\nabla)^{\leq 1}\Gamma_{a}\cup\{r\overline{\rho}\}.

Remark 2.15
.

The justification of Definition
2.14
has to do with the expected decay properties of the Ricci coefficients, see Lemma
4.1
.

Remark 2.16
.

In the sequel, we choose the following conventions:

•

For a quantity
h
satisfying the same or even better decay as
\Gamma_{i}
, for
i=g,b,a
, we write

h\in\Gamma_{i},\quad i=g,b,a.

•

For a sum of schematic notations, we ignore the terms which have same or even better decay. For example, we write

\Gamma_{g}+\Gamma_{b}=\Gamma_{b},\qquad\Gamma_{g}+\Gamma_{a}=\Gamma_{a},

since
\Gamma_{g}
has better decay than
\Gamma_{b}
and
\Gamma_{a}
throughout this paper.

2.6
Null structure equations

We recall the null structure equations for a double null foliation, see for example (3.1)-(3.5) of
[
17
]
.

Proposition 2.17
.

We have the null structure equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla_{4}\eta&=-\chi\cdot(\eta-\underline{\eta})-\beta,\\
\nabla_{3}\underline{\eta}&=-\underline{\chi}\cdot(\underline{\eta}-\eta)+\underline{\beta},\\
\nabla_{4}\widehat{\chi}+(\tr\chi)\widehat{\chi}&=-2\omega\widehat{\chi}-\alpha,\\
\nabla_{4}\tr\chi+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\chi)^{2}&=-|\widehat{\chi}|^{2}-2\omega\tr\chi,\\
\nabla_{3}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}+(\tr\underline{\chi})\widehat{\underline{\chi}}&=-2\underline{\omega}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}-\underline{\alpha},\\
\nabla_{3}\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\underline{\chi})^{2}&=-|\widehat{\underline{\chi}}|^{2}-2\underline{\omega}\tr\underline{\chi},\\
\nabla_{4}\widehat{\underline{\chi}}+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\chi)\widehat{\underline{\chi}}&=\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\eta}+2\omega\widehat{\underline{\chi}}-\frac{1}{2}(\tr\underline{\chi})\widehat{\chi}+\underline{\eta}\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\eta},\\
\nabla_{3}\widehat{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\underline{\chi})\widehat{\chi}&=\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\eta+2\underline{\omega}\widehat{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}(\tr\chi)\widehat{\underline{\chi}}+\eta\widehat{\otimes}\eta,\\
\nabla_{4}\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\chi)\tr\underline{\chi}&=2\omega\tr\underline{\chi}+2\rho-\widehat{\chi}\cdot\widehat{}\underline{\chi}+2\sdiv\underline{\eta}+2|\underline{\eta}|^{2},\\
\nabla_{3}\tr\chi+\frac{1}{2}(\tr\underline{\chi})\tr\chi&=2\underline{\omega}\tr\chi+2\rho-\widehat{\chi}\cdot\widehat{}\underline{\chi}+2\sdiv\eta+2|\eta|^{2}.\end{split}

(2.25)

We have the Codazzi equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sdiv\widehat{\chi}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla\tr\chi-\zeta\cdot\left(\widehat{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\right)-\beta,\\
\sdiv\widehat{}\underline{\chi}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla\tr\underline{\chi}+\zeta\cdot\left(\widehat{}\underline{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\right)+\underline{\beta},\end{split}

(2.26)

the torsion equation:

\curl\eta=-\curl\underline{\eta}=\sigma+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{}\underline{\chi}\wedge\widehat{\chi},

(2.27)

and the Gauss equation:

\mathbf{K}=-\frac{1}{4}\tr\chi\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{}\underline{\chi}\cdot\widehat{\chi}-\rho.

(2.28)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla_{4}\underline{\omega}&=2\omega\underline{\omega}+\frac{3}{4}|\eta-\underline{\eta}|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot(\eta+\underline{\eta})-\frac{1}{8}|\eta+\underline{\eta}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho,\\
\nabla_{3}\omega&=2\omega\underline{\omega}+\frac{3}{4}|\eta-\underline{\eta}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(\eta-\underline{\eta})\cdot(\eta+\underline{\eta})-\frac{1}{8}|\eta+\underline{\eta}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\rho.\end{split}

(2.29)

Proof.

See Section 7.4 of
[
5
]
.
∎

2.7
Bianchi equations

We recall the Bianchi equations in double null foliation, see Proposition 3.2.4 of
[
14
]
.

Proposition 2.18
.

In a double null frame, the Bianchi equations take the form

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\underline{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\underline{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\beta}+h[\underline{\alpha}_{4}],

\displaystyle h[\underline{\alpha}_{4}]

\displaystyle=4\omega\underline{\alpha}-3(\widehat{}\underline{\chi}\rho-{{}^{*}\widehat{}\underline{\chi}}\sigma)+(\zeta-4\underline{\eta})\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\beta},

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\underline{\beta}+2\tr\underline{\chi}\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=-\sdiv\underline{\alpha}+h[\underline{\beta}_{3}],

\displaystyle h[\underline{\beta}_{3}]

\displaystyle=-2\underline{\omega}\underline{\beta}+(2\zeta-\eta)\cdot\underline{\alpha},

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\underline{\beta}+\tr\chi\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\rho+{{}^{*}\nabla\sigma}+h[\underline{\beta}_{4}],

\displaystyle h[\underline{\beta}_{4}]

\displaystyle=2\omega\underline{\beta}+2\widehat{}\underline{\chi}\cdot\beta-3(\underline{\eta}\rho-{{}^{*}\underline{\eta}}\sigma),

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\rho+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\rho

\displaystyle=-\sdiv\underline{\beta}+h[\rho_{3}],

\displaystyle h[\rho_{3}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\alpha}+\zeta\cdot\underline{\beta}-2\eta\cdot\underline{\beta},

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\rho+\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi\rho

\displaystyle=\sdiv\beta+h[\rho_{4}],

\displaystyle h[\rho_{4}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{}\underline{\chi}\cdot\alpha+\zeta\cdot\beta+2\underline{\eta}\cdot\beta,

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\sigma+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\sigma

\displaystyle=-\sdiv{{}^{*}\underline{\beta}}+h[\sigma_{3}],

\displaystyle h[\sigma_{3}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\chi}\cdot{{}^{*}\underline{\alpha}}-\zeta\cdot{{}^{*}\underline{\beta}}+2\eta\cdot{{}^{*}\underline{\beta}},

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\sigma+\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi\sigma

\displaystyle=-\sdiv{{}^{*}\beta}+h[\sigma_{4}],

\displaystyle h[\sigma_{4}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\widehat{}\underline{\chi}\cdot{{}^{*}\alpha}-\zeta\cdot{{}^{*}\beta}-2\underline{\eta}\cdot{{}^{*}\beta},

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\beta+\tr\underline{\chi}\beta

\displaystyle=\nabla\rho+{{}^{*}\nabla}\sigma+h[\beta_{3}],

\displaystyle h[\beta_{3}]

\displaystyle=2\underline{\omega}\beta+2\widehat{\chi}\cdot\underline{\beta}+3(\eta\rho+{{}^{*}\eta}\sigma),

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\beta+2\tr\chi\beta

\displaystyle=\sdiv\alpha+h[\beta_{4}],

\displaystyle h[\beta_{4}]

\displaystyle=-2\omega\beta+(2\zeta+\underline{\eta})\alpha,

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\alpha

\displaystyle=\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\beta+h[\alpha_{3}],

\displaystyle h[\alpha_{3}]

\displaystyle=4\underline{\omega}\alpha-3(\widehat{\chi}\rho+{{}^{*}\widehat{\chi}}\sigma)+(\zeta+4\eta)\widehat{\otimes}\beta.

We also derive equations for
\widecheck{\rho}
and
\overline{\rho}
.

Lemma 2.19
.

We have the following equations:

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\underline{\beta}+\tr\chi\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\widecheck{\rho}+{{}^{*}\nabla\sigma}+h[\underline{\beta}_{4}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\beta+\tr\underline{\chi}\beta

\displaystyle=\nabla\widecheck{\rho}+{{}^{*}\nabla}\sigma+h[\beta_{3}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\widecheck{\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\widecheck{\rho}

\displaystyle=-\sdiv\underline{\beta}+h[\widecheck{\rho}_{3}],

\displaystyle h[\widecheck{\rho}_{3}]

\displaystyle:=\Gamma_{g}\cdot\underline{\beta}+h[\rho_{3}]-\overline{h[\rho_{3}]},

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\overline{\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\overline{\rho}

\displaystyle=h[\overline{\rho}_{3}],

\displaystyle h[\overline{\rho}_{3}]

\displaystyle:=\Gamma_{g}\cdot\underline{\beta}+\overline{h[\rho_{3}]},

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\widecheck{\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi\widecheck{\rho}

\displaystyle=\sdiv\beta+h[\widecheck{\rho}_{4}],

\displaystyle h[\widecheck{\rho}_{4}]

\displaystyle:=\Gamma_{g}\cdot\widecheck{\rho}+h[\rho_{4}]-\overline{h[\rho_{4}]},

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\overline{\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi\overline{\rho}

\displaystyle=h[\overline{\rho}_{4}],

\displaystyle h[\overline{\rho}_{4}]

\displaystyle:=\Gamma_{g}\cdot\widecheck{\rho}+\overline{h[\rho_{4}]},

where
h[\underline{\beta}_{4}]
,
h[\beta_{3}]
,
h[\rho_{3}]
and
h[\rho_{4}]
are defined in Proposition
2.18
.

Proof.

Note that
\nabla\widecheck{\rho}=\nabla\rho
, hence the first two equations follow directly from Proposition
2.18
. On the other hand, applying Corollary
2.5
, we infer

\displaystyle\Omega\nabla_{3}\overline{\rho}

\displaystyle=\overline{\widecheck{\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}}\,\widecheck{\rho}}+\overline{\Omega\nabla_{3}\rho}

\displaystyle=\overline{-\frac{3}{2}\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}\rho-\Omega\sdiv\underline{\beta}+h[\rho_{3}]}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\widecheck{\rho}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}\,\overline{\rho}+\overline{h[\rho_{3}]}+\overline{\underline{\beta}\cdot\nabla\Omega}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\widecheck{\rho},

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}\,\overline{\rho}+\overline{h[\rho_{3}]}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\underline{\beta}.

So, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\overline{\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\overline{\rho}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{g}\cdot\underline{\beta}+\overline{h[\rho_{3}]},

which implies the fourth equation. Combining with the equation of
\nabla_{3}\rho
in Proposition
2.18
, we infer

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\widecheck{\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\widecheck{\rho}=-\sdiv\underline{\beta}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\underline{\beta}+h[\rho_{3}]-\overline{h[\rho_{3}]},

which implies the third equation. The proof of the fifth and sixth equations are similar. This concludes the proof of Lemma
2.19
.
∎

We rewrite the Bianchi equations of Proposition
2.18
and Lemma
2.19
in the following Corollary.

Corollary 2.20
.

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\underline{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\tr\chi\underline{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\underline{\beta}+h[\underline{\alpha}_{4}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\underline{\beta}+2\tr\underline{\chi}\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=-\sdiv\underline{\alpha}+h[\underline{\beta}_{3}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\underline{\beta}+\tr\chi\underline{\beta}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\widecheck{\rho}+{{}^{*}\nabla\sigma}+h[\underline{\beta}_{4}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\widecheck{\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\widecheck{\rho}

\displaystyle=-\sdiv\underline{\beta}+h[\widecheck{\rho}_{3}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\widecheck{\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi\widecheck{\rho}

\displaystyle=\sdiv\beta+h[\widecheck{\rho}_{4}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\overline{\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\overline{\rho}

\displaystyle=h[\overline{\rho}_{3}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\overline{\rho}+\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi\overline{\rho}

\displaystyle=h[\overline{\rho}_{4}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\sigma+\frac{3}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\sigma

\displaystyle=-\sdiv{{}^{*}\underline{\beta}}+h[\sigma_{3}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\sigma+\frac{3}{2}\tr\chi\sigma

\displaystyle=-\sdiv{{}^{*}\beta}+h[\sigma_{4}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\beta+\tr\underline{\chi}\beta

\displaystyle=\nabla\widecheck{\rho}+{{}^{*}\nabla}\sigma+h[\beta_{3}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{4}\beta+2\tr\chi\beta

\displaystyle=\sdiv\alpha+h[\beta_{4}],

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\alpha

\displaystyle=\nabla\widehat{\otimes}\beta+h[\alpha_{3}],

where
h[\underline{\alpha}_{4}]
,
h[\underline{\beta}_{3}]
, … ,
h[\alpha_{3}]
are defined in Proposition
2.18
and Lemma
2.19
.

2.8
Commutation identities

We recall the following commutation identities for scalar functions, see Proposition 4.8.1 in
[
14
]
.

Proposition 2.21
.

For any scalar function
f
, we have:

\displaystyle[\nabla_{4},\nabla]f

\displaystyle=-\chi\cdot\nabla f+(\nabla\log\Omega)\nabla_{4}f,

\displaystyle[\nabla_{3},\nabla]f

\displaystyle=-\underline{\chi}\cdot\nabla f+(\nabla\log\Omega)\nabla_{3}f,

\displaystyle[\nabla_{4},\nabla_{3}]f

\displaystyle=2\omega\nabla_{3}f-2\underline{\omega}\nabla_{4}f-4\zeta\cdot\nabla f.

The following corollary is a direct consequence of Proposition
2.21
.

Corollary 2.22
.

For any scalar function
f
, we have:

\displaystyle[\Omega\nabla_{4},\nabla]f

\displaystyle=-\Omega\chi\cdot\nabla f,

\displaystyle[\Omega\nabla_{3},\nabla]f

\displaystyle=-\Omega\underline{\chi}\cdot\nabla f,

\displaystyle[\Omega\nabla_{4},r\nabla]f

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{g}\cdot r\nabla f,

\displaystyle[\Omega\nabla_{3},r\nabla]f

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{b}\cdot r\nabla f.

We also need the commutation identities for more general tensor fields. For this, we record the following commutation lemma.

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}
be an
S
-tangent
k
-covariant tensor on
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
. Then

\displaystyle[\nabla_{4},\nabla_{B}]U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{BC}\nabla_{C}U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}+F_{4BA_{1}...A_{k}},

\displaystyle F_{4BA_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle:=(\zeta_{B}+\underline{\eta}_{B})\nabla_{4}U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\chi_{A_{i}B}\,\underline{\eta}_{C}-\chi_{BC}\,\underline{\eta}_{A_{i}}+\in_{A_{i}C}{{}^{*}\beta}_{B})U_{A_{1}...C...A_{k}},

\displaystyle[\nabla_{3},\nabla_{B}]U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle=-\underline{\chi}_{BC}\nabla_{C}U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}+F_{3BA_{1}...A_{k}},

\displaystyle F_{3BA_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle:=(\eta_{B}-\zeta_{B})\nabla_{3}U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\underline{\chi}_{A_{i}B}\,\eta_{C}-\underline{\chi}_{BC}\,\eta_{A_{i}}+\in_{A_{i}C}{{}^{*}\underline{\beta}}_{B})U_{A_{1}...C...A_{k}},

\displaystyle[\nabla_{3},\nabla_{4}]U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle=F_{34A_{1}...A_{k}},

\displaystyle F_{34A_{1}...A_{k}}

\displaystyle:=-2\omega\nabla_{3}U+2\underline{\omega}\nabla_{4}U+4\zeta_{B}\nabla_{B}U_{A_{1}...A_{k}}+2\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\underline{\eta}_{A_{i}}\,\eta_{C}-\underline{\eta}_{A_{i}}\,\eta_{C}+\in_{A_{i}C}\sigma)U_{A_{1}...C...A_{k}}.

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Lemma 7.3.3 in
[
5
]
.
∎

Notice that Lemma
2.23
implies the following proposition.

Proposition 2.24
.

We have the following simple schematic consequences of the commutator identities

\displaystyle[r\nabla,\Omega\nabla_{4}]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{g}\cdot r\nabla+\Gamma_{g}^{(1)},

\displaystyle[r\nabla,\Omega\nabla_{3}]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{b}\cdot r\nabla+\Gamma_{b}^{(1)},

\displaystyle[\Omega\nabla_{4},\Omega\nabla_{3}]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{g}\cdot\nabla+r^{-1}\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}.

2.9
Teukolsky equations

We state the following
Teukolsky equations
first derived by Teukolsky in the linearized setting in
[
28
]
.

Proposition 2.25
.

We have the following Teukolsky equations for
\alpha
and
\underline{\alpha}
:

\begin{split}\Omega\nabla_{3}(\Omega\nabla_{4}(r\Omega^{2}\alpha))+2r\Omega^{2}d_{2}^{*}d_{2}(\Omega^{2}\alpha)&=-\frac{2\Omega}{r}\nabla_{3}(r\Omega^{2}\alpha)+(\Gamma_{g}\cdot(\beta,\alpha))^{(1)},\\
\Omega\nabla_{4}(\Omega\nabla_{3}(r\Omega^{2}\underline{\alpha}))+2r\Omega^{2}d_{2}^{*}d_{2}(\Omega^{2}\underline{\alpha})&=\frac{2\Omega}{r}\nabla_{4}(r\Omega^{2}\underline{\alpha})+(\Gamma_{b}\cdot(\underline{\alpha},\underline{\beta}))^{(1)}.\end{split}

(2.30)

Proof.

See for example Propositions 3.4.6 and 3.4.7 in
[
6
]
.
4
4

4

Remark that
\Omega=1+O(\epsilon)
in
[
6
]
while
\Omega=\frac{1}{2}+O(\epsilon)
in this paper.

∎

Lemma 2.26
.

We define the following quantities:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathring{\alpha}&:=\frac{1}{r^{4}}\nabla_{4}(r^{5}\alpha)\in\mathfrak{s}_{2},\qquad\quad\not{\alpha}:=rd_{2}\alpha\in\mathfrak{s}_{1},\\
\mathring{\underline{\alpha}}&:=\frac{1}{r^{4}}\nabla_{3}(r^{5}\underline{\alpha})\in\mathfrak{s}_{2},\qquad\quad\underline{\not{\alpha}}:=rd_{2}\underline{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{s}_{1}.\end{split}

(2.31)

Then, we have the following equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla_{3}\mathring{\alpha}&=-2d_{2}^{*}\not{\alpha}+\frac{4\alpha}{r}+\Gamma_{a}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)^{(1)}+\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}\cdot(\beta,\alpha),\\
\nabla_{4}\not{\alpha}+\frac{5}{2}\tr\chi\,\not{\alpha}&=d_{2}\mathring{\alpha}+\Gamma_{a}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)^{(1)}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\mathring{\alpha}+\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}\cdot(\beta,\alpha),\end{split}

(2.32)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla_{4}\mathring{\underline{\alpha}}&=-2d_{2}^{*}\underline{\not{\alpha}}+\frac{4\underline{\alpha}}{r}+\Gamma_{a}\cdot(\underline{\beta},\underline{\alpha})^{(1)}+(\Gamma_{b}\cdot(\underline{\beta},\underline{\alpha}))^{(1)},\\
\nabla_{3}\underline{\not{\alpha}}+\frac{5}{2}\tr\underline{\chi}\,\underline{\not{\alpha}}&=d_{2}\mathring{\underline{\alpha}}+\Gamma_{a}\cdot(\underline{\beta},\underline{\alpha})^{(1)}+\Gamma_{b}\cdot\mathring{\underline{\alpha}}+(\Gamma_{b}\cdot(\underline{\beta},\underline{\alpha}))^{(1)}.\end{split}

(2.33)

Proof.

We have from (
2.30
)

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}(\nabla_{4}(r\alpha))+2rd_{2}^{*}d_{2}\alpha=-\frac{4}{r}\nabla_{3}(r\alpha)+\Gamma_{a}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)^{(1)}+\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}\cdot(\beta,\alpha).

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{3}\mathring{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{3}(r^{-4}\nabla_{4}(r^{5}\alpha))

\displaystyle=\nabla_{3}(r^{-4}(4r^{3}e_{4}(r)r\alpha+r^{4}\nabla_{4}(r\alpha))

\displaystyle=\nabla_{3}(4\alpha+\nabla_{4}(r\alpha))+\Gamma_{a}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)^{(1)}+\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{r}r\nabla_{3}\alpha+\nabla_{3}\nabla_{4}(r\alpha)+\Gamma_{a}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)^{(1)}+\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{r}\nabla_{3}(r\alpha)-4e_{3}(r)\frac{\alpha}{r}-2rd_{2}^{*}d_{2}\alpha-\frac{4}{r}\nabla_{3}(r\alpha)+\Gamma_{a}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)^{(1)}+\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\alpha}{r}-2d_{2}^{*}\not{\alpha}+\Gamma_{a}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)^{(1)}+\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}\cdot(\beta,\alpha),

which implies the first equation in (
2.32
). Applying Proposition
2.24
, we have

\displaystyle\Omega\nabla_{4}\not{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\Omega\nabla_{4}\left(r^{-5}(rd_{2}(r^{5}\alpha))\right)

\displaystyle=-5r^{-6}\Omega e_{4}(r)r^{5}(rd_{2})\alpha+r^{-5}\Omega\nabla_{4}rd_{2}(r^{5}\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{5\Omega}{r}rd_{2}\alpha+r^{-1}rd_{2}r^{-4}(\Omega\nabla_{4}(r^{5}\alpha))+\Gamma_{a}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)^{(1)}+\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{5}{2}\Omega\tr\chi\,\not{\alpha}+\Omega d_{2}\mathring{\alpha}+\Gamma_{a}\cdot(\beta,\alpha)^{(1)}+\Gamma_{g}\cdot\mathring{\alpha}+\Gamma_{g}^{(1)}\cdot(\beta,\alpha),

which implies the second equation in (
2.32
). The proof of (
2.33
) is similar. This concludes the proof of Lemma
2.26
.
∎

2.10
Sobolev inequalities

Definition 2.27
.

For a tensor field
h
on a null cone
C
, we define its
L^{2}
–flux:

\|h\|_{2,C}:=\left(\int_{C}|h|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2.34)

We recall the following Sobolev inequalities.

Proposition 2.28
.

Let
F
be a tensor field, tangent to
S:=S(u,{\underline{u}})
at every point. We have the following estimates:

\displaystyle|rF|_{4,S}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|F\|_{2,C_{u}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})}+\|r\nabla F\|_{2,C_{u}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})}+\|r\nabla_{4}F\|_{2,C_{u}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle|r^{\frac{1}{2}}|u|^{\frac{1}{2}}F|_{4,S}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|F\|_{2,{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})}+\|r\nabla F\|_{2,{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})}+|u|\|\nabla_{3}F\|_{2,{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})}.

Proof.

See Corollary 3.2.1.1 in
[
5
]
and Corollary 4.1.1 in
[
14
]
.
∎

We also recall the following standard Sobolev inequalities.

Proposition 2.29
.

Let
F
be a tensor field on a
2
–sphere
S
. Then, we have

\sup_{S}r^{\frac{1}{2}}|F|\lesssim|F|_{4,S}+|r\nabla F|_{4,S}.

Proof.

See Lemma 4.1.3 of
[
14
]
.
∎

2.11
Evolution lemma

We recall the following evolution lemma, which will be used repeatedly in Sections
5
and
6.3
.

Lemma 2.30

(Evolution lemma)
.

Consider the spacetime
\mathcal{K}
foliated by a double null foliation
(u,{\underline{u}})
. Assume that, for
\epsilon>0
small enough, we have

|\Gamma_{g}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{r^{2}}.

(2.35)

Then, the following holds:

1.

Let
U,F
be
k
-covariant
S
-tangent tensor fields satisfying the outgoing evolution equation

\nabla_{4}U+\lambda_{0}\tr\chi\,U=F,

(2.36)

where
\lambda_{0}\geq 0
. Denoting
\lambda_{1}=2(\lambda_{0}-\frac{1}{p})
, we have along
C_{u}

|r^{\lambda_{1}}U|_{p,S}(u,{\underline{u}})\lesssim|r^{\lambda_{1}}U|_{p,S}(u,{\underline{u}}_{*})+\int_{{\underline{u}}}^{{\underline{u}}_{*}}|r^{\lambda_{1}}F|_{p,S}(u,{\underline{u}}^{\prime})d{\underline{u}}^{\prime}.

(2.37)

Here
{\underline{u}}_{*}
is the value that the function
{\underline{u}}(p)
assumes on
{\underline{C}}_{*}
.

2.

Let
V,\underline{F}
be k-covariant and S-tangent tensor fields satisfying the incoming evolution equation

\nabla_{3}V+\lambda_{0}\tr\underline{\chi}\,V=\underline{F}.

(2.38)

Denoting
\lambda_{1}=2(\lambda_{0}-\frac{1}{p})
, we have along
{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}

|r^{\lambda_{1}}V|_{p,S}(u,{\underline{u}})\lesssim|r^{\lambda_{1}}V|_{p,S(u_{0}({\underline{u}}),{\underline{u}})}+\int_{u_{0}({\underline{u}})}^{u}|r^{\lambda_{1}}\underline{F}|_{p,S}(u^{\prime},{\underline{u}})du^{\prime},

(2.39)

where
S(u_{0}({\underline{u}}),{\underline{u}})=C_{u_{0}({\underline{u}})}\cap{{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}}
and
u_{0}({\underline{u}})
is the unique value of
u
such that
S(u,{\underline{u}})
is in the future of
\Sigma_{0}
and touches
\Sigma_{0}
.

Proof.

See Lemma 4.1.5 in
[
14
]
.
∎

3
Main theorem

3.1
Fundamental norms

Our result holds for
s
–asymptotically flat initial data sets in the sense of Definition
1.2
with
s>3
. We will focus on the case
s\in[4,6]
in sections
3
to
6
, and we postpone the necessary adaptations to the case
s\in(3,4)
and
s>6
to Appendices
B
and
C
. The norms in sections
3.1.1
-
3.1.4
are defined with respect to the foliation
(u,{\underline{u}})
in
\mathcal{K}
while the norms in section
3.1.5
are defined with respect to the foliation
(u_{(0)},{\underline{u}})
in
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
.

3.1.1
\mathcal{R}
norms (curvature)

We define the norms of Ricci coefficients in the bootstrap region
\mathcal{K}
. We denote

\mathcal{R}_{0}^{S}:=\mathcal{R}_{0}^{S}[\alpha]+\mathcal{R}_{0}^{S}[\beta],\qquad\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}:=\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}[\beta]+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}[\widecheck{\rho},\sigma]+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}[\underline{\beta}]+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}[\underline{\alpha}]+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}[\overline{\rho}],

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0}^{S}[\alpha]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\sup_{p\in[2,4]}|r^{\frac{s+3}{2}-\frac{2}{p}}\alpha|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0}^{S}[\beta]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\sup_{p\in[2,4]}|r^{\frac{7}{2}-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-4}{2}}\beta|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}[\beta]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\sup_{p\in[2,4]}|r^{\frac{s+2}{2}-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{1}{2}}\beta|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}[\widecheck{\rho},\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\sup_{p\in[2,4]}|r^{3-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-3}{2}}(\widecheck{\rho},\sigma)|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}[\underline{\beta}]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\sup_{p\in[2,4]}|r^{2-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-1}{2}}\underline{\beta}|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}[\underline{\alpha}]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\sup_{p\in[2,4]}|r^{1-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s+1}{2}}\underline{\alpha}|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}[\overline{\rho}]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathcal{K}}|r^{3}\overline{\rho}|,

where the
L^{p}
–norms are defined in Definition
2.12
.
Then we define the norms of flux of curvature components. We denote

\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{R}_{[1]}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{[1]},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{[1]}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{R}_{0}+\mathcal{R}_{0}^{S}+\mathcal{R}_{1},\qquad\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{[1]}:=\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{1},

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathcal{R}_{0}[\alpha]^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{0}[\beta]^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{0}[(\widecheck{\rho},\sigma)]^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{0}[\underline{\beta}]^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\left(\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}[\beta]^{2}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}[(\widecheck{\rho},\sigma)]^{2}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}[\underline{\beta}]^{2}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}[\underline{\alpha}]^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathcal{R}_{1}[\alpha]^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{1}[\beta]^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{1}[(\widecheck{\rho},\sigma)]^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{1}[\underline{\beta}]^{2}+\mathcal{R}_{1}[\alpha_{4}]^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\left(\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{1}[\beta]^{2}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{1}[(\widecheck{\rho},\sigma)]^{2}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{1}[\underline{\beta}]^{2}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{1}[\underline{\alpha}]^{2}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{1}[\underline{\alpha}_{3}]^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0,1}[w]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{R}_{0,1}[w](u,{\underline{u}}),

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0,1}[w]

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0,1}[w](u,{\underline{u}}).

It remains to define
\mathcal{R}_{q}[w](u,{\underline{u}})
and
\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{q}[w](u,{\underline{u}})
for
q=0,1
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{q}[\alpha](u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=\|r^{\frac{s}{2}}(r\nabla)^{q}\alpha\|_{2,C_{u}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{q}[\beta](u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=\|r^{2}|u|^{\frac{s-4}{2}}(r\nabla)^{q}\beta\|_{2,C_{u}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{q}[(\widecheck{\rho},\sigma)](u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=\|r|u|^{\frac{s-2}{2}}(r\nabla)^{q}(\widecheck{\rho},\sigma)\|_{2,C_{u}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{q}[\underline{\beta}](u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=\||u|^{\frac{s}{2}}(r\nabla)^{q}\underline{\beta}\|_{2,C_{u}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{q}[\beta](u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=\|r^{\frac{s}{2}}(r\nabla)^{q}\beta\|_{2,{{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{q}[(\widecheck{\rho},\sigma)](u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|u|^{\frac{s-4}{2}}\|r^{2}(r\nabla)^{q}(\widecheck{\rho},\sigma)\|_{2,{{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{q}[\underline{\beta}](u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|u|^{\frac{s-2}{2}}\|r(r\nabla)^{q}\underline{\beta}\|_{2,{{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{q}[\underline{\alpha}](u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|u|^{\frac{s}{2}}\|(r\nabla)^{q}\underline{\alpha}\|_{2,{{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}[\alpha_{4}](u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=\|r^{\frac{s+2}{2}}\nabla_{4}\alpha\|_{2,C_{u}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{1}[\underline{\alpha}_{3}](u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|u|^{\frac{s+2}{2}}\|\nabla_{3}\underline{\alpha}\|_{2,{{\underline{C}}_{\underline{u}}}\cap V(u,{\underline{u}})}.

3.1.2
\mathcal{O}
norms (Ricci coefficients)

We define the norms of Ricci coefficients in the bootstrap region
\mathcal{K}
. Denoting

\mathcal{O}:=\mathcal{O}_{[1]}+\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{[1]},

(3.1)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{[1]}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{O}_{1}+\mathcal{O}_{0}+\mathcal{O}_{0}(\Omega\underline{\omega})+\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\left|r^{2}\left(\overline{\tr\chi}-\frac{2}{r}\right)\right|+\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\left|r\left(\Omega-\frac{1}{2}\right)\right|,

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{[1]}

\displaystyle:=\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{1}+\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{0}+\mathcal{O}_{0}(\Omega\omega)+\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\left|r|u|\left(\overline{\tr\underline{\chi}}+\frac{2}{r}\right)\right|.

We define
\mathcal{O}_{q}
and
\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{q}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{O}_{q}(\widecheck{\Omega\tr\chi})+\mathcal{O}_{q}(\widehat{\chi})+\mathcal{O}_{q}(\eta)+\mathcal{O}_{q}(\widecheck{\Omega\underline{\omega}})+\mathcal{O}_{q}(\widecheck{\Omega}),\qquad\,\,q=0,1,

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{q}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{O}_{q}(\widecheck{\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}})+\mathcal{O}_{q}(\widehat{\underline{\chi}})+\mathcal{O}_{q}(\underline{\eta})+\mathcal{O}_{q}(\widecheck{\Omega\omega}),\qquad\qquad\qquad\;q=0,1,

where

\mathcal{O}_{q}(\Gamma):=\sup_{p\in[2,4]}\sup_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\Gamma)(u,{\underline{u}}).

It remains to define
\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\Gamma)(u,{\underline{u}})
for
q=0,1
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\widecheck{\Omega\tr\chi})(u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{2+q-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-3}{2}}\nabla^{q}\widecheck{\Omega\tr\chi}|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\widecheck{\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}})(u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{2+q-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-3}{2}}\nabla^{q}\widecheck{\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}}|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\widehat{\chi})(u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{2+q-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-3}{2}}\nabla^{q}\widehat{\chi}|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\widehat{}\underline{\chi})(u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{1+q-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-1}{2}}\nabla^{q}\widehat{}\underline{\chi}|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\eta)(u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{2+q-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-3}{2}}\nabla^{q}\eta|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\underline{\eta})(u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{2+q-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-3}{2}}\nabla^{q}\underline{\eta}|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{0}^{p,S}(\Omega\omega)(u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{2-\frac{2}{p}}\Omega\omega|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{0}^{p,S}(\Omega\underline{\omega})(u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{2-\frac{2}{p}}\Omega\underline{\omega}|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\widecheck{\Omega\omega})(u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{2+\frac{s-3}{6}-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-3}{3}}(r\nabla)^{q}\widecheck{\Omega\omega}|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\widecheck{\Omega\underline{\omega}})(u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{1-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-1}{2}}(r\nabla)^{q}\widecheck{\Omega\underline{\omega}}|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\widecheck{\Omega})(u,{\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{1-\frac{2}{p}}|u|^{\frac{s-3}{2}}(r\nabla)^{q}\widecheck{\Omega}|_{p,S(u,{\underline{u}})}.

We also define

\displaystyle\breve{\mathcal{O}}:=\sum_{q=0}^{1}\left(\mathcal{O}_{q}(\widecheck{\Omega\tr\chi})+\mathcal{O}_{q}(\eta)+\mathcal{O}_{q}(\widecheck{\Omega})\right)+\sup_{\mathcal{K}}r\left|\Omega-\frac{1}{2}\right|+\sup_{\mathcal{K}}r^{2}\left|\overline{\Omega\tr\chi}-\frac{1}{r}\right|,

which appears in sections

and

.

-norms and

The following norms are defined on the union of spheres

\underline{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K):=\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{1}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)+\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{0}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)+\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{0}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)(\Omega\omega)+\sup_{{\underline{u}}}\left|r^{2}\left(\Omega\tr\underline{\chi}+\frac{1}{r}\right)\right|,

with

\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{q}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)(\Gamma):=\sup_{p\in[2,4]}\sup_{{\underline{u}}}\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)(\Gamma)({\underline{u}}).

It remains to define

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)(\widecheck{\Omega^{-1}\tr\underline{\chi}})({\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{\frac{s+1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}}(r\nabla)^{q}\widecheck{\Omega^{-1}\tr\underline{\chi}}|_{p,S(u_{0}({\underline{u}}),{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{0}^{p,S}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)(\Omega\omega)({\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{2-\frac{2}{p}}\Omega\omega|_{p,S(u_{0}({\underline{u}}),{\underline{u}})},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{p,S}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)(\widecheck{\Omega\omega})({\underline{u}})

\displaystyle:=|r^{\frac{s+1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}}(r\nabla)^{q}\widecheck{\Omega\omega}|_{p,S(u_{0}({\underline{u}}),{\underline{u}})}.

We also define

\breve{\underline{\mathcal{O}}}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K):=\sum_{q=0}^{1}\left(\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{q}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)(\widecheck{\Omega^{-1}\tr\underline{\chi}})+\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{q}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)(\widecheck{\Omega\omega})\right)+\underline{\mathcal{O}}_{0}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)(\Omega\omega),

which appears in sections

and

.

We can extend the foliation
(u,{\underline{u}})
to a neighborhood of
\Sigma_{0}\setminus K
in

\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{2}:=\int_{\Sigma_{0}\setminus K}\sum_{l=0}^{1}{r}^{s}\left(|{\mathfrak{d}}^{l}\alpha|^{2}+|{\mathfrak{d}}^{l}\beta|^{2}+|{\mathfrak{d}}^{l}(\widecheck{\rho},\sigma)|^{2}+|{\mathfrak{d}}^{l}\underline{\beta}|^{2}+|{\mathfrak{d}}^{l}\underline{\alpha}|^{2}\right)+\sup_{\Sigma_{0}\setminus K}|{r}^{3}\overline{\rho}|^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{q}^{*p,S}(\zeta)

3.1.5
\mathcal{O}_{(0)}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{(0)}

\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
)

In this section, all the norms are defined by the initial layer foliation
(u_{(0)},{\underline{u}})
in
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{(0)}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\Gamma_{(0)}}\mathcal{O}_{(0)}(\Gamma_{(0)})+\sup_{\mathcal{K}_{(0)}}\left|r_{(0)}^{2}\left(\tr\chi_{(0)}-\frac{2}{r_{(0)}}\right)\right|+\sup_{\mathcal{K}_{(0)}}\left|r_{(0)}^{2}\left(\tr\underline{\chi}_{(0)}+\frac{2}{r_{(0)}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle+\sup_{\mathcal{K}_{(0)}}\left|r_{(0)}^{2}\omega_{(0)}\right|+\sup_{\mathcal{K}_{(0)}}\left|r_{(0)}^{2}\underline{\omega}_{(0)}\right|+\sup_{\mathcal{K}_{(0)}}\left|r_{(0)}\left(\Omega_{(0)}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\right|,

where

\mathcal{O}_{(0)}(\Gamma_{(0)}):=\sum_{q=0}^{2}\sup_{\mathcal{K}_{(0)}}\sup_{p\in[2,4]}\left|r_{(0)}^{\frac{s+1}{2}-\frac{2}{p}}(r_{(0)}\nabla)^{q}\Gamma_{(0)}\right|_{p,S_{(0)}(u_{(0)},{\underline{u}})},

with

\Gamma_{(0)}\in\{\widecheck{\tr\chi}_{(0)},\widecheck{\tr\underline{\chi}}_{(0)},\widehat{\chi}_{(0)},\widehat{\underline{\chi}}_{(0)},\eta_{(0)},\underline{\eta}_{(0)},\widecheck{\omega}_{(0)},\widecheck{\underline{\omega}}_{(0)}\}.

We define the curvature flux on the initial hypersurface:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{(0)}^{2}:=\sum_{l=0}^{2}\int_{\Sigma_{0}\setminus K}r_{(0)}^{s}\left|{\mathfrak{d}}_{(0)}^{l}\left(\alpha_{(0)},\beta_{(0)},\widecheck{\rho}_{(0)},\sigma_{(0)},\underline{\beta}_{(0)},\underline{\alpha}_{(0)}\right)\right|^{2}+\sup_{\Sigma_{0}\setminus K}|r_{(0)}^{3}{\rho_{(0)}}|,

where

{\mathfrak{d}}_{(0)}:=\{r_{(0)}\nabla_{3},r_{(0)}\nabla_{4},r_{(0)}\nabla\}.

(3.3)

3.1.6
\osc
–norms (Oscillation control)

We have two foliations
(u,{\underline{u}})
and
(u_{(0)},{\underline{u}})
in the initial layer
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
which will be compared using
\osc
–norms. We denote
(f,\lambda)
the change of frame from
(u,{\underline{u}})
to
(u_{(0)},{\underline{u}})
, defined in Lemma
6.1
in section
6.1.1
. We define

\displaystyle\osc:=\osc(f)+\osc(\lambda)+\osc(r),

where

\displaystyle\osc(f)=\sup_{\mathcal{K}_{(0)}}\left|r^{\frac{s-1}{2}}{\mathfrak{d}}^{\leq 1}f\right|,\qquad\osc(\lambda)=\sup_{\mathcal{K}_{(0)}}r|\overset{\circ}{\lambda}|,\qquad\osc(r):=\sup_{\mathcal{K}_{(0)}}|r_{(0)}-r|,

with

\displaystyle\overset{\circ}{\lambda}:=\lambda-1,\qquad{\mathfrak{d}}:=\{r\nabla_{3},r\nabla_{4},r\nabla\}.

See Definition
6.9
in section
6.4
for a generalized definition of
\osc
–norms.

3.2
Main theorem

The goal of this paper is to prove the following theorem, which provides a new proof of the seminal result obtained by Klainerman and Nicolò in
[
14
]
.

Theorem 3.1

(Main Theorem)
.

Consider an initial data set
(\Sigma_{0},g,k)

s
–asymptotically flat in the sense of Definition
1.2
with
s>3
. Assume that we have the following control of the initial layer region
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
defined in section
2.1.2

\mathcal{O}_{(0)}\leq\epsilon_{0},\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{(0)}\leq\epsilon_{0},

(3.4)

where
\mathcal{O}_{(0)}
,
\mathfrak{R}_{(0)}
are defined in section
3.1.5
and
\epsilon_{0}>0
is small enough.

Then, the initial layer
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}
has a unique development
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
in its future domain of dependence with the following properties:

1.

(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{g})
can be foliated by a double null foliation
(u,{\underline{u}})
. Moreover, the outgoing cones
C_{u}
are complete for all
u\leq u_{0}
.

2.

The norms
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{R}
defined in section
3.1
for
s\in[4,6]
, and in sections
B.1
and
C.1
respectively for
s\in(3,4)
and
s>6
satisfy

\mathcal{O}\lesssim\epsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{R}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}.

(3.5)

Remark 3.2
.

Theorem
3.1
contains also a number of important conclusions following from (
3.5
): peeling properties, complete future infinity, Bondi mass formula and so on, see Chapter 8 in
[
14
]
.

Remark 3.3
.

Theorem
3.1
is proved in section
3.4
for
s\in[4,6]
, and extended to
s\in(3,4)
and
s>6
in Appendices
B
and
C
.

The proof of Theorem
3.1
is given in section
3.4
. It hinges on a sequence of basic theorems stated in section
3.3
, concerning estimates for
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{R}
norms.

We choose
\epsilon_{0}
and
\epsilon
small enough such that

\epsilon\ll\delta_{0}<1,\qquad\epsilon:=\epsilon_{0}^{\frac{2}{3}},

where we recall that
\delta_{0}
is the height of the initial layer, see (
2.14
). Here,
A\ll B
means that
CA<B
where
C
is the largest universal constant among all the constants involved in the proof via
\lesssim
.

3.3
Main intermediate results

The following lemma provides comparisons between
r_{(0)},r,w,u_{(0)},{\underline{u}},u
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Under the assumptions

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{(0)}\leq\epsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{O}\leq\epsilon,\qquad\osc\leq\epsilon,

(3.6)

we have in the initial layer region
\mathcal{K}_{(0)}

\displaystyle|r_{(0)}-w|\lesssim\epsilon_{0}\log r_{(0)},\qquad|u_{(0)}-u|\lesssim\epsilon\log r_{(0)},

(3.7)

and in the bootstrap region
\mathcal{K}

\left|r-\frac{{\underline{u}}-u}{2}\right|\lesssim\epsilon\log r.

(3.8)

Proof.

See Appendix
A
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Recall that we have
|u|\leq{\underline{u}}
, and
u<0
in
\mathcal{K}
. Together with (
3.8
), this yields

|u|\leq{\underline{u}}\simeq r\quad\mbox{ in }\mathcal{K}.

(3.9)

In the sequel, we will use (
3.7
), (
3.8
) and (
3.9
) frequently without explicitly mentioning them.

The following theorems are important intermediate steps in the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Theorem M0
.

Under the assumptions

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{(0)}\leq\epsilon_{0},\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{(0)}\leq\epsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{O}\leq\epsilon,\qquad\osc\leq\epsilon,

we have

\mathfrak{R}_{0}\lesssim\epsilon_{0},\qquad\breve{\underline{\mathcal{O}}}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)\lesssim\epsilon_{0}.

(3.10)

If in addition we assume that

\breve{\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0},

then, we have

\osc\lesssim\epsilon_{0},\qquad\underline{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)\lesssim\epsilon_{0}.

(3.11)

Theorem M0 is proved in section
6.2
. The proof is based on null frame transformation formulae introduced by Klainerman and Szeftel in
[
19
]
, see Proposition
6.2
in section
6.1.1
.

Remark 3.6
.

The second part of Theorem M0 implies that we need an estimate for
\breve{\mathcal{O}}
to control
\osc
. To this end, we first estimate the quantities in
\breve{\underline{\mathcal{O}}}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)
, which are nonlinearly dependent on
\osc
. Next, we apply them as initial data to estimate part of the Ricci coefficients, i.e.
\breve{\mathcal{O}}
, see (
3.17
) in Theorem M3. Then, we obtain the oscillation control, which allows us to estimate all the initial data, i.e. the control (
3.11
) for
\underline{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)
.

Theorem M1
.

Assume that

\mathcal{O}_{(0)}\leq\epsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{O}\leq\epsilon,\qquad\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}\leq\epsilon.

(3.12)

Then, we have

\mathcal{R}\lesssim\mathfrak{R}_{0}.

(3.13)

Theorem M1 is proved in section
4
. The proof is based on the
r^{p}
–weighted method introduced by Dafermos and Rodnianski in
[
7
]
.

Theorem M2
.

Assume that

\mathcal{O}^{*}({\underline{C}}_{*})\leq\epsilon,\qquad\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}\leq\Delta_{0},\qquad\mathcal{O}_{(0)}(\Sigma_{0}\cap{\underline{C}}_{*})\leq\mathcal{I}_{0}.

(3.14)

Then, we have

\mathcal{O}^{*}({\underline{C}}_{*})\lesssim\Delta_{0}+\mathcal{I}_{0}+\epsilon^{2}.

(3.15)

Theorem M2 is proved in section
6.3
. The proof is done by integrating the transport equations along the null generator
\underline{L}
of the last slice
{\underline{C}}_{*}
and applying elliptic estimates on
2
–spheres of the geodesic foliation of
{\underline{C}}_{*}
.

Theorem M3
.

Assume that

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\leq\epsilon,\qquad\mathcal{R}_{0}^{S}+\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{S}\leq\Delta_{0},\qquad\mathcal{O}^{*}({\underline{C}}_{*})\leq\mathcal{I}_{*},\qquad\breve{\underline{\mathcal{O}}}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)\leq\breve{\mathcal{I}}_{0},

(3.16)

then, we have

\breve{\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\breve{\mathcal{I}}_{0}+\mathcal{I}_{*}+\Delta_{0}+\epsilon^{2}.

(3.17)

If we assume in addition that

\underline{\mathcal{O}}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)\leq\mathcal{I}_{0},

(3.18)

then, we have

\mathcal{O}\lesssim\mathcal{I}_{0}+\mathcal{I}_{*}+\Delta_{0}+\epsilon^{2}.

(3.19)

Theorem M3 is proved in section
5
. The proof is done by integrating the transport equations along the outgoing and incoming null cones and applying elliptic estimate on
2
–spheres of the double null foliation of the spacetime
\mathcal{K}
.

Theorem M4
.

We consider the spacetime
\mathcal{K}
and its double null foliation
(u,{\underline{u}})
, which satisfy the hypotheses

\mathcal{O}\lesssim\epsilon_{0},\qquad\mathcal{R}\lesssim\epsilon_{0},\qquad\osc\lesssim\epsilon_{0}.

(3.20)

Then, we can extend the spacetime
\mathcal{K}=V(u_{0},{\underline{u}}_{*})
and the double null foliation
(u,{\underline{u}})
to a new spacetime
\wideparen{\mathcal{K}}=\wideparen{V}(u_{0},{\underline{u}}_{*}+\nu)
, where
\nu
is sufficiently small, and an associated double null foliation
(\wideparen{u},{\underline{u}})
. Moreover, the new foliation
(\wideparen{u},{\underline{u}})
is geodesic on the new last slice
{\underline{C}}_{**}:={\underline{C}}_{{\underline{u}}_{*}+\nu}
and
the new norms satisfy

\displaystyle\wideparen{\mathcal{O}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0},\qquad\wideparen{\mathcal{R}}\lesssim\epsilon_{0},\qquad\wideparen{\osc}\lesssim\epsilon_{0}.

(3.21)

Theorem M4 is proved in section
6.4
. The proof is based on local existence, and the null frame transformation formulas of Proposition
6.2
. We also need to reapply Theorems M1, M2 and M3 in the extended spacetime region
\wideparen{\mathcal{K}}
.

Remark 3.7
.

When compared to
[
14
]
, we have the following similarities and differences:

1.

We treat the general decay
s>3
in Theorem
3.1
while the main result of
[
14
]
6
6

6

We also reobtain the strong peeling properties of
[
15
]
that correspond to
s>7
.
correspond to the particular case
s=4
in Theorem
3.1
.

2.

In
[
14
]
, third order derivatives of Ricci coefficients and second order derivatives of curvature are estimated. In this paper, we only estimate first order derivative of both Ricci coefficients and curvature respectively in Theorems M0-M2-M3 and M1.

3.

To estimate the curvature components in Theorem M1, instead of using vectorfield method as in
[
14
]
, we use the
r^{p}
–weighted estimates introduced by Dafermos and Rodnianski in
[
7
]
.

4.

On the last slice
{\underline{C}}_{*}
, a canonical foliation is used in
[
14
]
while we use a geodesic foliation in Theorem M2.

5.

The estimates of Ricci coefficients and the extension argument, i.e. the proof of Theorems M3 and M4, are similar to
[
14
]
.

3.4
Proof of the main theorem

We now use Theorems M0-M4 to prove Theorem
3.1
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\aleph({\underline{u}}_{*})
the set of spacetimes
\mathcal{K}
associated with a double null foliation
(u,{\underline{u}})
which satisfy the following properties:

1.

\mathcal{K}=V(u_{0},{\underline{u}}_{*})
.

2.

The foliation on
{\underline{C}}_{*}
is geodesic.

3.

We have the following bootstrap bounds:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\leq\epsilon,\qquad\mathcal{R}\leq\epsilon,\qquad\osc\leq\epsilon.

(3.22)

Definition 3.9
.

We denote
\mathcal{U}
the set of values
{\underline{u}}_{*}
such that
\aleph({\underline{u}}_{*})\neq\emptyset
.

Applying the local existence result of Theorem 10.2.1 in
[
5
]
and the assumption
\mathcal{O}_{(0)}\leq\epsilon_{0}
, we deduce that (
3.22
) holds if
{\underline{u}}_{*}
is sufficiently small. So, we have
\mathcal{U}\neq\emptyset
.

Define
{\underline{u}}_{*}
to be the supremum of the set
\mathcal{U}
. We want to prove
{\underline{u}}_{*}=+\infty
. We assume by contradiction that
{\underline{u}}_{*}
is finite. In particular we may assume
{\underline{u}}_{*}\in\mathcal{U}
. We consider the region
\mathcal{K}=V(u_{0},{\underline{u}}_{*})
. Recall that we have

\mathcal{O}_{(0)}\leq\epsilon_{0},\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{(0)}\leq\epsilon_{0},

(3.23)

according to the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
. Applying Theorems M0 and M1, we obtain

\mathcal{R}\lesssim\mathfrak{R}_{0}\lesssim\epsilon_{0},\qquad\breve{\underline{\mathcal{O}}}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus K)\lesssim\epsilon_{0}.

(3.24)

Then, applying Theorem M2 and the hypothesis
\mathcal{O}\leq\epsilon
, we obtain
7
7

7

By definition,
\mathcal{O}\leq\epsilon
implies
\mathcal{O}^{*}({\underline{C}}_{*})\leq\epsilon
.

\mathcal{O}^{*}({\underline{C}}_{*})\lesssim\epsilon_{0}.

In view of the above, the quantities in Theorem M3 satisfy the following conditions:
